Meredith began classes the day after the troops arrived.[^cite_note-113]

Kennard and other activists continued to work on public university desegregation. In 1965, [Raylawni Branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raylawni_Branch) and [Gwendolyn Elaine Armstrong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gwendolyn_Elaine_Armstrong) became the first African-American students to attend the [University of Southern Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Southern_Mississippi). By that time, McCain helped ensure they had a peaceful entry.[^cite_note-sketch-114] In 2006, Judge Robert Helfrich ruled that Kennard was factually innocent of all charges for which he had been convicted in the 1950s.[^cite_note-Kennard-108]

### Albany Movement, 1961–1962

Main article: [Albany Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albany_Movement)

The SCLC, which some student activists had criticized for its failure to participate more fully in the freedom rides, committed much of its prestige and resources to a desegregation campaign in [Albany, Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albany,_Georgia), in November 1961. King, who had been criticized personally by some SNCC activists for his distance from the dangers that local organizers faced—and given the derisive nickname "De Lawd" as a result—intervened personally to assist the campaign led by both SNCC organizers and local leaders.

The campaign was a failure because of the cunning tactics of [Laurie Pritchett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurie_Pritchett), the local police chief, and divisions within the black community. The goals may not have been specific enough. Pritchett contained the marchers without resorting to violent attacks on demonstrators that would inflame national opinion. He also arranged for arrested demonstrators to be taken to jails in surrounding communities, allowing plenty of room to remain in his jail. Pritchett also foresaw King's presence as a danger and forced his release to prevent King from rallying the black community. King left in 1962 without having achieved any dramatic victories. The local movement, however, continued the struggle and made significant gains over the next few years.[^cite_note-115]

### Birmingham campaign, 1963

Main article: [Birmingham campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_campaign)

See also: [Children's Crusade (1963)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Crusade_\(1963\))

The Albany Movement served as an important lesson for the SCLC, which undertook the Birmingham Campaign in 1963. Executive Director [Wyatt Tee Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyatt_Tee_Walker) carefully planned the campaign's early strategy and tactics. It focused on one goal—the desegregation of Birmingham's downtown merchants, rather than total desegregation, as in Albany.

The movement's efforts were aided by the brutal response of local authorities, particularly [Eugene "Bull" Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bull_Connor), the Commissioner of Public Safety. He had long held much political power but had lost a recent election for mayor to a less rabidly segregationist candidate. Refusing to accept the new mayor's authority, Connor intended to stay in office.

The campaign used a variety of nonviolent methods of confrontation, including sit-ins, kneel-ins at local churches, and a march to the county building to mark the beginning of a voter-registration drive. The city, however, obtained an [injunction](39343.md) barring all such protests. Convinced that the order was unconstitutional, the campaign defied it and prepared for [mass arrests](28079790.md) of its supporters. King elected to be among those arrested on April 12, 1963.[^cite_note-116]



Recreation of Martin Luther King Jr.'s cell in Birmingham Jail at the [National Civil Rights Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Civil_Rights_Museum)

While in jail, King wrote his famous "[Letter from Birmingham Jail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letter_from_Birmingham_Jail)"[^cite_note-117] on the margins of a newspaper, since he had not been allowed any writing paper while held in solitary confinement.[^cite_note-118] Supporters appealed to the Kennedy administration, which intervened to obtain King's release. [Walter Reuther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Reuther), president of the [United Auto Workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Auto_Workers), arranged for \$160,000 to bail out King and his fellow protestors.[^cite_note-119] King was allowed to call his wife, who was recuperating at home after the birth of their fourth child, and was released early on April 19.

The campaign, however, faltered as it ran out of demonstrators willing to risk arrest. [James Bevel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bevel), SCLC's Director of Direct Action and Director of Nonviolent Education, then came up with a bold and controversial alternative: to train high school students to take part in the demonstrations. As a result, in what would be called the [Children's Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Crusade_\(1963\)), more than 1,000 students skipped school on May 2 to meet at the 16th Street Baptist Church and join the demonstrations. More than six hundred marched out of the church, fifty at a time, in an attempt to walk to City Hall to speak to Birmingham's mayor about segregation. They were arrested and jailed. In this first encounter, the police acted with restraint. The next day, however, another one thousand students gathered at the church. When Bevel started them marching fifty at a time, Bull Connor finally unleashed police dogs on them and then turned the city's fire hoses on the children. National television networks broadcast the scenes of the dogs attacking demonstrators and the water from the fire hoses knocking down the schoolchildren.[^cite_note-120]

Widespread public outrage led the [Kennedy administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_John_F._Kennedy) to intervene more forcefully in negotiations between the white business community and the SCLC. On May 10, the parties announced an agreement to desegregate the lunch counters and other public accommodations downtown, to create a committee to eliminate discriminatory hiring practices, to arrange for the release of jailed protesters, and to establish regular means of communication between black and white leaders.

A black and white photograph of a building in ruins next to an intact wall

Wreckage at the Gaston Motel following the [bomb explosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_crisis) on May 11, 1963

Not everyone in the black community approved of the agreement—[Fred Shuttlesworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_Shuttlesworth) was particularly critical, since he was skeptical about the good faith of Birmingham's power structure from his experience in dealing with them. Parts of the white community reacted violently. They [bombed the Gaston Motel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_riot_of_1963#Gaston_Motel), which housed the SCLC's unofficial headquarters, and the home of King's brother, the Reverend A. D. King. In response, [thousands of Black people rioted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_crisis), burning numerous buildings, and one of them stabbed and wounded a police officer.[^cite_note-121]



Alabama governor [George Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Wallace) [tried to block desegregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stand_in_the_Schoolhouse_Door) at the [University of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama) and is confronted by U.S. Deputy Attorney General [Nicholas Katzenbach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Katzenbach) in 1963.

Kennedy was prepared to federalize the [Alabama National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama_National_Guard) if the need arose. Four months later, on September 15, a conspiracy of Ku Klux Klan members [bombed the Sixteenth Street Baptist Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_Street_Baptist_Church_bombing) in Birmingham, killing four young girls.

### "Rising tide of discontent" and Kennedy's response, 1963

Main articles: [Gloria Richardson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_Richardson), [Stand in the Schoolhouse Door](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stand_in_the_Schoolhouse_Door), and [Civil Rights Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Address)

Birmingham was only one of over a hundred cities rocked by the chaotic protests that spring and summer, some in the North but mainly in the South. During the March on Washington, Martin Luther King Jr. would refer to such protests as "the whirlwinds of revolt." In Chicago, Black people rioted through the South Side in late May after a white police officer shot a fourteen-year-old black boy who was fleeing the scene of a robbery.[^cite_note-Nicholas_Andrew_Bryant_2006_pg._2-122] Violent clashes between black activists and white workers took place in both Philadelphia and Harlem in successful efforts to integrate state construction projects.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124] On June 6, over a thousand whites attacked a sit-in in Lexington, North Carolina; Black people fought back and one white man was killed.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] Edwin C. Berry of the National Urban League warned of a complete breakdown in race relations: "My message from the beer gardens and the barbershops all indicate the fact that the Negro is ready for war."[^cite_note-Nicholas_Andrew_Bryant_2006_pg._2-122]

In [Cambridge, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge,_Maryland), a working‐class city on the [Eastern Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Shore_of_Maryland), [Gloria Richardson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_Richardson) of SNCC led a movement that pressed for desegregation but also demanded low‐rent public housing, job‐training, public and private jobs, and an end to police brutality.[^cite_note-Jackson167-127] On June 11, struggles between Black people and whites [escalated into violent rioting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_riot_of_1963), leading Maryland Governor [J. Millard Tawes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Millard_Tawes) to declare [martial law](50477.md). When negotiations between Richardson and Maryland officials faltered, Attorney General Robert F. Kennedy directly intervened to negotiate a desegregation agreement.[^cite_note-128] Richardson felt that the increasing participation of poor and working-class Black people was expanding both the power and parameters of the movement, asserting that "the people as a whole really do have more intelligence than a few of their leaders.ʺ[^cite_note-Jackson167-127]

&#x20;In their deliberations during this wave of protests, the Kennedy administration privately felt that militant demonstrations were ʺbad for the countryʺ and that "Negroes are going to push this thing too far."[^cite_note-web1.millercenter.org-129] On May 24, Robert Kennedy had a [meeting with prominent black intellectuals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin-Kennedy_meeting) to discuss the racial situation. The black delegation criticized Kennedy harshly for vacillating on civil rights and said that the African-American community's thoughts were increasingly turning to violence. The meeting ended with ill will on all sides.[^cite_note-130][^cite_note-131][^cite_note-Schlesinger333-132] Nonetheless, the Kennedys ultimately decided that new legislation for equal public accommodations was essential to drive activists "into the courts and out of the streets."[^cite_note-web1.millercenter.org-129][^cite_note-133]&#x20;



The March on Washington for Jobs and Freedom at the [National Mall](167796.md)



Leaders of the March on Washington posing before the Lincoln Memorial on August 28, 1963

On June 11, 1963, [George Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Wallace), Governor of Alabama, tried [to block](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stand_in_the_Schoolhouse_Door)[^cite_note-134] the integration of the [University of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama). President John F. Kennedy sent a military force to make Governor Wallace step aside, allowing the enrollment of [Vivian Malone Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivian_Malone_Jones) and [James Hood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Hood). That evening, President Kennedy addressed the nation on TV and radio with his historic [civil rights speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Address), where he lamented "a rising tide of discontent that threatens the public safety." He called on Congress to pass new civil rights legislation, and urged the country to embrace civil rights as "a moral issue...in our daily lives."[^cite_note-135] In the early hours of June 12, [Medgar Evers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medgar_Evers), field secretary of the Mississippi NAACP, was assassinated by a member of the Klan.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] The next week, as promised, on June 19, 1963, President Kennedy submitted his Civil Rights bill to Congress.[^cite_note-abbeville-138]

### March on Washington, 1963

Main article: [March on Washington for Jobs and Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_for_Jobs_and_Freedom)



[Bayard Rustin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayard_Rustin) *(left)* and [Cleveland Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleveland_Robinson) *(right)*, organizers of the March, on August 7, 1963

Randolph and [Bayard Rustin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bayard_Rustin) were the chief planners of the [March on Washington for Jobs and Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_for_Jobs_and_Freedom), which they proposed in 1962. In 1963, the Kennedy administration initially opposed the march, concerned that it would undermine the drive to pass civil rights legislation. However, Randolph and King were firm in their belief that the march would proceed.[^cite_note-139] With the march going forward, the Kennedys decided it was important to work to ensure its success. Concerned about the turnout, President Kennedy enlisted the aid of white church leaders and [Walter Reuther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Reuther), president of the [UAW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Automobile_Workers), to help mobilize white supporters for the march.[^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141]

The march was held on August 28, 1963. Unlike the planned 1941 march, in which Randolph included only black-led organizations, the 1963 march was a collaborative effort involving all the major civil rights organizations, the more progressive wing of the labor movement, and other liberal organizations. The march had six official goals:

- meaningful civil rights laws

- a massive federal works program

- full and fair employment

- decent housing

- the right to vote

- adequate integrated education.

Of these, the march's primary focus was the passage of the Civil Rights Act that the Kennedy administration had proposed following the upheavals in Birmingham.



Martin Luther King Jr. at a civil rights march on Washington, D.C.

National media attention also greatly contributed to the march's national exposure and probable impact. In the essay "The March on Washington and Television News",[^cite_note-southernspaces.org-142] historian William Thomas notes: "Over five hundred cameramen, technicians, and correspondents from the major networks were set to cover the event. More cameras would be set up than had filmed the last presidential inauguration. One camera was positioned high in the Washington Monument, to give dramatic vistas of the marchers". By carrying the organizers' speeches and offering their own commentary, television stations framed the way their local audiences saw and understood the event.[^cite_note-southernspaces.org-142]

The march was a success, although not without controversy. An estimated 200,000 to 300,000 demonstrators gathered in front of the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial), where King delivered his famous "[I Have a Dream](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I_Have_a_Dream)" speech. While many speakers applauded the Kennedy administration for the efforts it had made toward obtaining new, more effective civil rights legislation protecting the right to vote and outlawing segregation, [John Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lewis) of [SNCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Nonviolent_Coordinating_Committee) took the administration to task for not doing more to protect southern Black people and civil rights workers under attack in the Deep South.

After the march, King and other civil rights leaders met with President Kennedy at the [White House](33057.md). While the Kennedy administration appeared sincerely committed to passing the bill, it was not clear that it had enough votes in Congress to do so. However, when [President Kennedy was assassinated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_F._Kennedy_assassination) on November 22, 1963,[^cite_note-abbeville-138] the new President [Lyndon Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) decided to use his influence in [Congress](31756.md) to bring about much of Kennedy's legislative agenda.

### St. Augustine, Florida, 1963–1964

Main article: [St. Augustine movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Augustine_movement)

Further information: [1964 Monson Motor Lodge protest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Monson_Motor_Lodge_protest)



"We Cater to White Trade Only" sign on a restaurant window in [Lancaster, Ohio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lancaster,_Ohio), in 1938. In 1964, [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) was arrested and spent a night in jail for attempting to eat at a white-only restaurant in [St. Augustine, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Augustine,_Florida).

In [St. Augustine, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Augustine,_Florida), a local movement had been picketing segregated local institutions since 1963. In the fall of 1964, four teenagers who came to be known as "The St. Augustine Four" sat in at a local Woolworth's lunch counter, seeking to get served. They were arrested and convicted of trespassing and sentenced to six months in jail and reform school. It took a special act of the governor and cabinet of Florida to release them after national protests by the *[Pittsburgh Courier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Courier)*, [Jackie Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackie_Robinson), and others.

Black and white photograph of segregationists fighting on a beach

White segregationists (foreground) trying to prevent black people from swimming at a "White only" beach in St. Augustine, Florida during the [1964 Monson Motor Lodge protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Monson_Motor_Lodge_protests)

In response to repression, the St. Augustine movement practiced armed self-defense alongside nonviolent direct action. In June 1963, Hayling publicly stated that "I and the others have armed. We will shoot first and answer questions later. We are not going to die like Medgar Evers." The comment made national headlines.[^cite_note-143] When Klan nightriders terrorized black neighborhoods in St. Augustine, Hayling's NAACP members often drove them off with gunfire. In October 1963, a Klansman was killed.[^cite_note-144]

In 1964, Hayling and other activists urged the [Southern Christian Leadership Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Christian_Leadership_Conference) to come to St. Augustine. Four prominent Massachusetts women—Mary Parkman Peabody, Esther Burgess, Hester Campbell (all of whose husbands were Episcopal bishops), and Florence Rowe (whose husband was vice president of an insurance company)—also came to lend their support. The arrest of Peabody, the 72-year-old mother of the governor of Massachusetts, for attempting to eat at the segregated Ponce de Leon Motor Lodge in an integrated group, made front-page news across the country and brought the movement in St. Augustine to the attention of the world.[^cite_note-145]

Widely publicized activities continued in the ensuing months. When King was arrested, he sent a "Letter from the St. Augustine Jail" to a northern supporter, [Rabbi](51273.md) [Israel S. Dresner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_S._Dresner). A week later, the largest mass arrest of rabbis in American history took place, while they were conducting a prayer-in at the segregated Monson Motel. A well-known photograph taken in St. Augustine shows [the manager of the Monson Motel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Monson_Motor_Lodge_protests) pouring [hydrochloric acid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrochloric_acid) in the swimming pool while Black people and whites are swimming in it. As he did so, he yelled that he was "cleaning the pool", a presumed reference to it now being, in his eyes, racially contaminated.[^cite_note-146] The photograph was run on the front page of a Washington newspaper the day the Senate was to vote on passing the Civil Rights Act of 1964.

### Chester school protests, Spring 1964

Main article: [Chester school protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester_school_protests)

From November 1963 through April 1964, the [Chester school protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester_school_protests) were a series of civil rights protests led by [George Raymond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Raymond) of the [National Association for the Advancement of Colored Persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_for_the_Advancement_of_Colored_Persons) (NAACP) and [Stanley Branche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanley_Branche) of the Committee for Freedom Now (CFFN) that made [Chester, Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chester,_Pennsylvania) one of the key northern sites of the civil rights movement. [James Farmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Farmer), the national director of the [Congress of Racial Equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Racial_Equality) called Chester "*the Birmingham of the North*".[^cite_note-Mele-147]

In 1962, Branche and the CFFN focused on improving conditions at the predominantly black Franklin Elementary school in Chester. Although the school was built to house 500 students, it had become overcrowded with 1,200 students. The school's average class size was 39, twice the number of nearby all-white schools.[^cite_note-Phoenix-148] The school was built in 1910 and had never been updated. Only two bathrooms were available for the entire school.[^cite_note-nvdbase-149] In November 1963, CFFN protesters blocked the entrance to Franklin Elementary school and the Chester Municipal Building, resulting in the arrest of 240 protesters. Following public attention to the protests stoked by media coverage of the mass arrests, the mayor and school board negotiated with the CFFN and NAACP.[^cite_note-Mele-147] The Chester Board of Education agreed to [reduce class sizes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Class-size_reduction) at Franklin school, remove unsanitary toilet facilities, relocate classes held in the boiler room and coal bin, and repair school grounds.[^cite_note-nvdbase-149]

Emboldened by the success of the Franklin Elementary School demonstrations, the CFFN recruited new members, sponsored voter-registration drives, and planned a citywide boycott of Chester schools. Branche built close ties with students at nearby [Swarthmore College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swarthmore_College), [Pennsylvania Military College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennsylvania_Military_College), and [Cheyney State College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheyney_State_College) to ensure large turnouts at demonstrations and protests.[^cite_note-Mele-147] Branche invited [Dick Gregory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Gregory) and [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X) to Chester to participate in the "Freedom Now Conference",[^cite_note-McLarnon-150] and other national civil rights leaders such as [Gloria Richardson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_Richardson) came to Chester in support of the demonstrations.[^cite_note-151]

In 1964, a series of almost nightly protests brought chaos to Chester as protestors argued that the Chester School Board had [de facto](43594.md) [segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_segregation) [of schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_segregation_in_the_United_States). The mayor of Chester, [James Gorbey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Gorbey), issued "The Police Position to Preserve the Public Peace", a ten-point statement promising an immediate return to law and order. The city deputized firefighters and trash collectors to help handle demonstrators.[^cite_note-Mele-147] The State of Pennsylvania deployed 50 state troopers to assist the 77-member Chester police force.[^cite_note-nvdbase-149] The demonstrations were marked by violence and charges of police brutality.[^cite_note-152] Over six hundred people were arrested over a two-month period of civil rights rallies, marches, pickets, boycotts and sit-ins.[^cite_note-Mele-147] Pennsylvania Governor [William Scranton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Scranton) became involved in the negotiations and convinced Branche to obey a court-ordered moratorium on demonstrations.[^cite_note-McLarnon-150] Scranton created the Pennsylvania Human Relations Commission to conduct hearings on the de facto segregation of public schools. All protests were discontinued while the commission held hearings during the summer of 1964.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMele201796-153]

In November 1964, the Pennsylvania Human Relations Commission concluded that the Chester School Board had violated the law and ordered the Chester School District to desegregate the city's six predominantly African-American schools. The city appealed the ruling, which delayed implementation.[^cite_note-nvdbase-149]

### Freedom Summer, 1964

Main article: [Freedom Summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Summer)

In the summer of 1964, [COFO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Federated_Organizations) brought nearly 1,000 activists to Mississippi—most of them white college students from the North and West—to join with local black activists to register voters, teach in "Freedom Schools", and organize the [Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Freedom_Democratic_Party) (MFDP).[^cite_note-crmvet.org-154]

Many of Mississippi's white residents deeply resented the outsiders and attempts to change their society. State and local governments, police, the [White Citizens' Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Citizens'_Council), and the Ku Klux Klan used arrests, beatings, arson, murder, spying, firing, evictions, and other forms of intimidation and harassment to oppose the project and prevent Black people from registering to vote or achieving social equality.[^cite_note-155]



[Missing persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missing_persons) poster created by the [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI) in 1964 [shows the photographs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murders_of_Chaney,_Goodman,_and_Schwerner) of [Andrew Goodman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Goodman_\(activist\)), [James Chaney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Chaney), and [Michael Schwerner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Schwerner)

On June 21, 1964, [three civil rights workers disappeared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murders_of_Chaney,_Goodman,_and_Schwerner): [James Chaney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Chaney), a young black Mississippian and plasterer's apprentice; and two [Jewish](25955086.md) activists, [Andrew Goodman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Goodman_\(activist\)), a [Queens College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queens_College,_City_University_of_New_York) anthropology student; and [Michael Schwerner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Schwerner), a [CORE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Racial_Equality) organizer from [Manhattan](45470.md)'s [Lower East Side](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_East_Side). They were found weeks later, murdered by conspirators who turned out to be local members of the Klan, some of the members of the [Neshoba County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neshoba_County,_Mississippi) sheriff's department. This outraged the public, leading the U.S. Justice Department, along with the FBI (the latter of which had previously avoided dealing with the issue of segregation and persecution of Black people), to take action. The outrage over these murders helped lead to the passage of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 and the Voting Rights Act of 1965.

From June to August, Freedom Summer activists worked in 38 local projects scattered across the state, with the largest number concentrated in the [Mississippi Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Delta) region. At least 30 Freedom Schools, with close to 3,500 students, were established, and 28 community centers were set up.[^cite_note-156]

Over the course of the Summer Project, some 17,000 Mississippi Black people attempted to become registered voters in defiance of the red tape and forces of [white supremacy](33178.md) arrayed against them—only 1,600 (less than 10%) succeeded. But more than 80,000 joined the [Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Freedom_Democratic_Party) (MFDP), founded as an alternative political organization, showing their desire to vote and participate in politics.[^cite_note-157]

Though [Freedom Summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Summer) failed to register many voters, it had a significant effect on the course of the civil rights movement. It helped break down the decades of people's isolation and repression that were the foundation of the [Jim Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_laws) system. Before Freedom Summer, the national news media had paid little attention to the persecution of black voters in the Deep South and the dangers endured by black civil rights workers. The progression of events throughout the South increased media attention to Mississippi.[^cite_note-crmvet1-158]

The deaths of affluent northern white students and threats to non-Southerners attracted the full attention of the media spotlight to the state. Many black activists became embittered, believing the media valued the lives of whites and Black people differently. Perhaps the most significant effect of Freedom Summer was on the volunteers, almost all of whom—black and white—still consider it to have been one of the defining periods of their lives.[^cite_note-crmvet1-158]

### Civil Rights Act of 1964

Main article: [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964)

Although President Kennedy had [proposed civil rights legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Address) and it had support from Northern Congressmen and Senators of both parties, Southern Senators blocked the bill by threatening [filibusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster). After considerable parliamentary maneuvering and 54 days of filibuster on the floor of the United States Senate, President Johnson got a bill through Congress.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEReeves1993521–524-159]



Lyndon B. Johnson signs the historic [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964)

On July 2, 1964, Johnson signed the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964),[^cite_note-cra64-106] which banned discrimination based on "race, color, religion, sex or national origin" in employment practices and public accommodations. The bill authorized the Attorney General to file lawsuits to enforce the new law. The law also nullified state and local laws that required such discrimination.

### Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party, 1964

Main article: [Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Freedom_Democratic_Party)

Black people in Mississippi had been [disfranchised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disfranchisement) by statutory and constitutional changes since the late 19th century. In 1963, COFO held a [Freedom Ballot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_Freedom_Ballot) in Mississippi to demonstrate black Mississippians' desire to vote. More than 80,000 people registered and voted in the mock election, which pitted an integrated slate of candidates from the "Freedom Party" against the official state Democratic Party candidates.[^cite_note-160]



President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) *(center)* meets with civil rights leaders [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md), [Whitney Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Whitney_Young), and [James Farmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_L._Farmer_Jr.), January 1964

In 1964, organizers launched the Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party (MFDP) to challenge the all-white official party. When Mississippi voting registrars refused to recognize their candidates, they held their own primary. They selected [Fannie Lou Hamer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fannie_Lou_Hamer), [Annie Devine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annie_Bell_Robinson_Devine), and [Victoria Gray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_Gray_Adams) to run for [Congress](31756.md), and a slate of delegates to represent Mississippi at the 1964 Democratic National Convention.[^cite_note-crmvet.org-154]

The presence of the Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party in [Atlantic City, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_City,_New_Jersey), was inconvenient, however, for the convention organizers. They had planned a triumphant celebration of the Johnson administration's civil rights achievements, rather than a fight over racism within the Democratic Party. All-white delegations from other Southern states threatened to walk out if the official slate from Mississippi was not seated. Johnson was worried about the inroads that Republican [Barry Goldwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Goldwater)'s campaign was making in what previously had been the white Democratic stronghold of the "Solid South", as well as the support that [George Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Wallace) had received in the North during the Democratic primaries.

Johnson could not, however, prevent the MFDP from taking its case to the Credentials Committee. There, [Fannie Lou Hamer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fannie_Lou_Hamer) testified eloquently about the beatings that she and others endured and the threats they faced for trying to register to vote. Turning to the television cameras, Hamer asked, "Is this America?"

Johnson offered the MFDP a "compromise" under which it would receive two non-voting, at-large seats, while the white delegation sent by the official Democratic Party would retain its seats. The MFDP angrily rejected the "compromise."

The MFDP kept up its agitation at the convention after it was denied official recognition. When all but three of the "regular" Mississippi delegates left because they refused to pledge allegiance to the party, the MFDP delegates borrowed passes from sympathetic delegates and took the seats vacated by the official Mississippi delegates. National party organizers removed them. When they returned the next day, they found convention organizers had removed the empty seats that had been there the day before. They stayed and sang "freedom songs".

The 1964 Democratic Party convention disillusioned many within the MFDP and the civil rights movement, but it did not destroy the MFDP. The MFDP became more radical after Atlantic City. It invited [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X) to speak at one of its conventions and opposed the [Vietnam War](32611.md).

### Selma Voting Rights Movement

Main articles: [Selma to Montgomery marches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selma_to_Montgomery_marches) and [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act)

[SNCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Nonviolent_Coordinating_Committee) had undertaken an ambitious voter registration program in [Selma, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selma,_Alabama), in 1963, but by 1965, little headway had been made in the face of opposition from Selma's sheriff, Jim Clark. After residents asked the SCLC for assistance, King came to Selma to lead several marches, at which he was arrested along with 250 other demonstrators. The marchers continued to meet violent resistance from the police. [Jimmie Lee Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jimmie_Lee_Jackson), a resident of nearby Marion, was killed by police at a later march on February 17, 1965. Jackson's death prompted [James Bevel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Bevel), director of the Selma Movement, to initiate and organize a plan to march from Selma to [Montgomery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery,_Alabama), the state capital.

On March 7, 1965, acting on Bevel's plan, [Hosea Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hosea_Williams) of the SCLC and John Lewis of SNCC led a march of 600 people to walk the 54 miles (87 km) from Selma to the state capital in Montgomery. Six blocks into the march, at the [Edmund Pettus Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmund_Pettus_Bridge) where the marchers left the city and moved into the county, state troopers, and local county law enforcement, some mounted on horseback, attacked the peaceful demonstrators with billy clubs, [tear gas](1202646.md), rubber tubes wrapped in barbed wire, and bullwhips. They drove the marchers back into Selma. Lewis was knocked unconscious and dragged to safety. At least 16 other marchers were hospitalized. Among those gassed and beaten was [Amelia Boynton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amelia_Boynton_Robinson), who was at the center of civil rights activity at the time.



Police attack nonviolent marchers on "Bloody Sunday", the first day of the [Selma to Montgomery marches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selma_to_Montgomery_marches).

The national broadcast of the news footage of law enforcement officers attacking unresisting marchers seeking to exercise their constitutional right to vote provoked a national response, and hundreds of people from all over the country came for a second march. King turned around these marchers at the last minute to avoid violating a federal injunction. This displeased many demonstrators, especially those who resented King's nonviolence (such as [James Forman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Forman) and [Robert F. Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Williams)).

That night, local Whites attacked [James Reeb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Reeb), a voting rights supporter. He died of his injuries in a Birmingham hospital on March 11. Due to the national outcry at a White minister being murdered so brazenly (as well as the subsequent civil disobedience led by Gorman and other SNCC leaders all over the country, especially in Montgomery and at the White House), the marchers were able to lift the injunction and obtain protection from federal troops, permitting them to make the march across Alabama without incident two weeks later; during the march, Gorman, Williams, and other more militant protesters carried bricks and sticks of their own.

Four Klansmen shot and killed [Detroit](8687.md) homemaker [Viola Liuzzo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viola_Liuzzo) as she drove marchers back to Selma that night.

### Voting Rights Act of 1965

Eight days after the first march, but before the final march, President Johnson delivered a televised address to support the voting rights bill he had sent to Congress. In it, he stated:

> Their cause must be our cause too. Because it is not just Negroes, but really it is all of us, who must overcome the crippling legacy of bigotry and injustice. And we shall overcome.

On May 26, the Senate passed S. 1564, the [Voting Rights Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965), by a vote of 77–19, with only Southern Senators opposing the bill.[^cite_note-Bending-161][^cite_note-162] On July 9, the House of Representatives passed H.R. 6400, the House version of the bill, by a vote of 333–85.[^cite_note-Bending-161]<sup>: 163–165 </sup>[^cite_note-163] On August 3–4, the two houses of Congress reconciled the two bill, and on August 6, President Johnson signed the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965). The bill suspended literacy tests and other subjective voter registration tests. It authorized Federal supervision of voter registration in states and individual voting districts where such tests were used and where African Americans were historically underrepresented on voting rolls relative to the eligible population. African Americans who had been barred from registering to vote finally had an alternative to taking suits to local or state courts, which had seldom prosecuted their cases successfully. If discrimination in voter registration occurred, the 1965 act authorized the [Attorney General of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attorney_General_of_the_United_States) to send Federal examiners to replace local registrars.

Within months of the bill's passage, 250,000 new black voters had been registered, one-third of them by federal examiners. Within four years, voter registration in the South had more than doubled. In 1965, Mississippi had the highest black voter turnout at 74% and led the nation in the number of black public officials elected. In 1969, Tennessee had a 92% turnout among black voters; Arkansas, 78%; and Texas, 73%.

Several whites who had opposed the Voting Rights Act paid a quick price. In 1966, [Sheriff Jim Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Clark_\(sheriff\)) of Selma, Alabama, infamous for using [cattle prods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cattle_prod) against civil rights marchers, was up for reelection. Although he took off the notorious "Never" pin on his uniform, he was defeated. At the election, Clark lost as Black people voted to get him out of office.

Black people regaining the power to vote changed the political landscape of the South. When Congress passed the Voting Rights Act, only about 100 African Americans held elective office, all in northern states. By 1989, there were more than 7,200 African Americans in office, including more than 4,800 in the South. Nearly every county in Alabama where the population was majority black had a black sheriff. Southern Black people held top positions in city, county, and state governments.

Atlanta elected a black mayor in 1982, [Andrew Young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Young), as did [Jackson, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson,_Mississippi) in 1997, with [Harvey Johnson Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvey_Johnson_Jr.), and [New Orleans](53842.md) in 1978, with [Ernest Morial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernest_Nathan_Morial). National-level Black politicians included [Barbara Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Jordan), elected as a Representative from Texas in 1973, and President Jimmy Carter appointed Andrew Young as [United States Ambassador to the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_the_United_Nations). [Julian Bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julian_Bond) was elected to the [Georgia State Legislature](426277.md) in 1965, although political reaction to his public [opposition to the U.S. involvement in the Vietnam War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opposition_to_the_U.S._involvement_in_the_Vietnam_War) prevented him from taking his seat until 1967. [John Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lewis) was first elected in 1986 to represent [Georgia's 5th congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia's_5th_congressional_district) in the [United States House of Representatives](19468510.md), where he served from 1987 until he died in 2020. There were only two [Black members of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_African-American_United_States_representatives) from the states of the former Confederacy [elected in 1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections), and four [elected in 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections), but this rose to 16 [in 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_United_States_House_of_Representatives_elections).

### Fair housing movements, 1966–1968

The first major blow against housing segregation in the era, the [Rumford Fair Housing Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumford_Fair_Housing_Act), was passed in [California](5407.md) in 1963. It was overturned by white California voters and real estate lobbyists the following year with [Proposition 14](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Proposition_14_\(1964\)), a move which helped precipitate the [Watts riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watts_riots).[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165] In 1966, the [California Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_California) invalidated Proposition 14 and reinstated the Rumford Fair Housing Act.[^cite_note-166]

Working and organizing for [fair housing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_housing) laws became a major project of the movement over the next two years, with Martin Luther King Jr., James Bevel, and [Al Raby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Raby) leading the [Chicago Freedom Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Freedom_Movement) around the issue in 1966. In the following year, Father [James Groppi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Groppi) and the [NAACP Youth Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP_Youth_Council) also attracted national attention with a fair housing campaign in Milwaukee.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] Both movements faced violent resistance from white homeowners and legal opposition from conservative politicians.

The Fair Housing Bill was the most contentious civil rights legislation of the era. Senator [Walter Mondale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Mondale), who advocated for the bill, noted that over successive years, it was the most [filibustered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster) legislation in U.S. history. It was opposed by most Northern and Southern senators, as well as the [National Association of Real Estate Boards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_of_Real_Estate_Boards). A proposed "Civil Rights Act of 1966" had collapsed completely because of its fair housing provision.[^cite_note-169] Mondale commented that:

> A lot of civil rights \[legislation] was about making the South behave and taking the teeth from George Wallace, \[but] this came right to the neighborhoods across the country. This was civil rights getting personal.[^cite_note-propublica.org-170]

### Nationwide riots, 1965–1967

Main article: [Ghetto riots (1964–1969)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghetto_riots_\(1964%E2%80%931969\))

See also: [Long Hot Summer of 1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Hot_Summer_of_1967)

Further information: [Watts riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watts_riots), [Detroit Riot of 1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detroit_Riot_of_1967), and [1967 Newark riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Newark_riots)

In the mid-1960s, the U.S. experienced a series of "long hot summers" of [civil unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghetto_riots_\(1964%E2%80%931969\)). While the early civil rights movement primarily focused on legal challenges to segregation in the South, the "long hot summers" brought attention to the racial disparities and issues within urban communities in the North.[^cite_note-171] Systemic racism, police brutality, high unemployment rates, poor living conditions in urban Black neighborhoods, and a sense of hopelessness contributed to the widespread unrest.[^cite_note-Sullivan2021-172][^cite_note-The_Riots_of_the_Long,_Hot_Summer-173][^cite_note-Ghettos-174]

#### Watts



Soldiers direct traffic away from an area of [South Central Los Angeles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Central_Los_Angeles) burning during the [1965 Watts riot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watts_Riots)

The momentum for the advancement of civil rights came to a sudden halt in August 1965 with [riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watts_Riots) in the [Watts district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watts,_Los_Angeles) of [Los Angeles](18110.md). The riots were ignited by the arrest of Marquette Frye during a traffic stop, which escalated into a physical confrontation with police officers and drew a large crowd of onlookers. During the six days of unrest, rioters engaged in widespread [looting](209811.md) of stores, burning buildings through [arson](184695.md), and in some cases, using [sniper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sniper) tactics to fire at authorities. To quell the violence, [National Guard](37988.md) troops were deployed to the area and imposed a [curfew](297681.md).[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-Oberschall-176]

After 34 people were killed and \$35 million (equivalent to \$357.58 million in 2025) in property was damaged, the public feared an expansion of the violence to other cities, and so the appetite for additional programs in President Lyndon Johnson's agenda was lost.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178]

#### Newark and Detroit



[1967 Newark riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Newark_riots)



Destroyed buildings during the [1967 Detroit riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Detroit_riots)

Occurring well after the conclusion of the 1966 Chicago open housing movement, in what is known as the "[Long hot summer of 1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_hot_summer_of_1967)" more than 150 riots erupted across the United States, with the most destructive occurring in [Detroit](8687.md), Michigan, and [Newark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newark,_New_Jersey), New Jersey.[^cite_note-179] The *[Boston Globe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_Globe)* called it "a revolution of black Americans against white Americans, a violent petition for the redress of long-standing grievances." The *Globe* asserted that [Great Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Society) legislation had affected little fundamental improvement.[^cite_note-Dallek_1998,_p._412-180]

The [Newark riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Newark_riots) were sparked by the arrest and beating of John William Smith, a Black cab driver, by police officers. The unrest lasted for five days, involving widespread looting, arson, and violent confrontations with police and National Guard troops. Some 26 people were killed, more than 700 were injured, and more than 1,000 residents were arrested.[^cite_note-Dallek_1998,_p._412-180][^cite_note-181]

In Detroit, a large [black middle class](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_middle_class) had begun to develop among those African Americans who worked at unionized jobs in the automotive industry. These workers complained of persisting racist practices, limiting the jobs they could have, and opportunities for promotion. The [United Auto Workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Auto_Workers) channeled these complaints into bureaucratic and ineffective grievance procedures.[^cite_note-182] Violent white mobs enforced the segregation of housing up through the 1960s.[^cite_note-pbs.org-183] The [Detroit riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_Detroit_riot) were sparked by a police raid on an unlicensed after-hours bar, commonly called the "Blind Pig," in a predominantly Black neighborhood. The riots lasted for five days, causing significant property damage, 1,200 injuries, and at least 43 deaths (33 of those killed were Black residents of the city).[^cite_note-The_Riots_of_the_Long,_Hot_Summer-173] Governor [George Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_W._Romney) sent in 7,400 National Guard troops to quell fire bombings, looting, and attacks on businesses and police. President Lyndon Johnson deployed [U.S. Army](32087.md) troops with [tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanks) and [machine guns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_guns). Residents reported that police officers and National Guardsmen shot at black civilians and suspects indiscriminately.[^cite_note-pbs.org-183][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcLaughlin20141–9,_40–41-185]

At an August 2, 1967 cabinet meeting, Attorney General [Ramsey Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramsey_Clark) warned that untrained and undisciplined local police forces and National Guardsmen might trigger a "[guerrilla war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guerrilla_war) in the streets," as evidenced by the climate of [sniper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sniper) fire in Newark and Detroit.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] Snipers were a significant element in many of the riots, creating a dangerous situation for both law enforcement and civilians, with shooters often targeting from rooftops and other concealed locations.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191]

#### Kerner Commission

The riots confounded many civil rights activists of both races, given the recent passage of major civil rights legislation. They also caused a backlash among Northern whites, many of whom stopped supporting civil rights causes.[^cite_note-192] President Johnson formed the National Advisory Commission on Civil Disorders, informally known as the [Kerner Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerner_Commission), on July 28, 1967, to explore the causes behind the recurring outbreaks of urban civil disorder.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-africanaonline-194] The commission's scope included the 164 disorders occurring in the first nine months of 1967. The president had directed them, in simple words, to document what happened, find out why it happened, and find out how to prevent it.[^cite_note-Johnson1967Remarks-195]

The commission's 1968 report identified police practices, unemployment and underemployment, and lack of adequate housing as the most significant grievances motivating the rage.[^cite_note-kerner-196] It suggested legislative measures to promote racial integration and alleviate poverty and concluded that the nation was "moving toward two societies, one black, one white—separate and unequal."[^cite_note-kernerreport-197] The president, fixated on the Vietnam War and keenly aware of budgetary constraints, barely acknowledged the report.[^cite_note-LHS67-198]

### Memphis, King assassination, and Civil Rights Act of 1968

Main articles: [Poor People's Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_People's_Campaign), [Assassination of Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.), and [Civil Rights Act of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1968)

See also: [King assassination riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_assassination_riots) and [Orangeburg massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orangeburg_massacre)



A 3,000-person shantytown called [Resurrection City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_People's_Campaign#Resurrection_City) was established in 1968 on the [National Mall](167796.md) as part of the Poor People's Campaign.

As 1968 began, the fair housing bill was being [filibustered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibustered) once again, but two developments revived it.[^cite_note-propublica.org-170] The [Kerner Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerner_Commission) report on the [1967 ghetto riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_hot_summer_of_1967) was delivered to Congress on March 1, and it strongly recommended "a comprehensive and enforceable federal open housing law" as a remedy to the civil disturbances. The Senate was moved to end its filibuster that week.[^cite_note-huduser.org-199]

[James Lawson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Lawson_\(American_activist\)) invited King to [Memphis, Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis,_Tennessee), in March 1968 to support a [sanitation workers' strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memphis_sanitation_strike). These workers launched a campaign for [union](17626.md) representation after two workers were accidentally killed on the job; they were seeking fair wages and improved working conditions. King considered their struggle to be a vital part of the [Poor People's Campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poor_People's_Campaign) he was planning.

A day after delivering his stirring "[I've Been to the Mountaintop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I've_Been_to_the_Mountaintop)" sermon, which has become famous for his vision of American society, King was assassinated on April 4, 1968, at the [Lorraine Motel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorraine_Motel) in Memphis. [Riots broke out](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_assassination_riots) in black neighborhoods in more than 110 cities across the United States in the days that followed, notably [in Chicago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Chicago_riots), [Baltimore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltimore_riot_of_1968), and [Washington, D.C.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Washington,_D.C.,_riots)

The day before [King's funeral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Funeral_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.), April 8, a completely silent march with [Coretta Scott King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coretta_Scott_King), [SCLC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Christian_Leadership_Conference), and UAW president [Walter Reuther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Reuther) attracted approximately 42,000 participants.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] Armed National Guardsmen lined the streets, sitting on [M-48 tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M-48_tanks), to protect the marchers, and helicopters circled overhead. On April 9, Mrs. King led another 150,000 people in a funeral procession through the streets of Atlanta.[^cite_note-202] Her dignity revived courage and hope in many of the Movement's members, confirming her place as the new leader in the struggle for racial equality.

Coretta Scott King said,[^cite_note-203]

> [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) gave his life for the poor of the world, the garbage workers of Memphis and the peasants of Vietnam. The day that Negro people and others in bondage are truly free, on the day want is abolished, on the day wars are no more, on that day I know my husband will rest in a long-deserved peace.



Aftermath of the [King assassination riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_assassination_riots) in Washington, D.C.

[Ralph Abernathy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Abernathy) succeeded King as the head of the SCLC and attempted to carry forth King's plan for a Poor People's March. It was to unite Black people and whites to campaign for fundamental changes in American society and economic structure. The march went forward under Abernathy's plainspoken leadership but did not achieve its goals.

#### Civil Rights Act of 1968

The [U.S. House of Representatives](19468510.md) had been deliberating its Fair Housing Act in early April, before King's assassination and the aforementioned [wave of unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_assassination_riots) that followed, the largest since the Civil War.[^cite_note-204] Senator [Charles Mathias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Mathias) wrote:

> \[S]ome Senators and Representatives publicly stated they would not be intimidated or rushed into legislating because of the disturbances. Nevertheless, the news coverage of the riots and the underlying disparities in income, jobs, housing, and education, between White and Black Americans helped educate citizens and Congress about the stark reality of an enormous social problem. Members of Congress knew they had to act to redress these imbalances in American life to fulfill the dream that King had so eloquently preached.[^cite_note-huduser.org-199]

The House passed the legislation on April 10, less than a week after King was murdered, and President Johnson signed it the next day. The [Civil Rights Act of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1968) prohibited discrimination concerning the sale, rental, and financing of housing based on race, religion, and national origin. It also made it a federal crime to "by force or by the threat of force, injure, intimidate, or interfere with anyone...by reason of their race, color, religion, or national origin."[^cite_note-205]

### *Gates v. Collier*



[Mississippi State Penitentiary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_State_Penitentiary)

Conditions at the [Mississippi State Penitentiary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_State_Penitentiary) at [Parchman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_State_Penitentiary), then known as Parchman Farm, became part of public civil rights discussions after [activists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Activism) were imprisoned there. In the spring of 1961, Freedom Riders came to the South to test the [desegregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desegregation_in_the_United_States) of public facilities. By the end of June 1963, Freedom Riders had been convicted in [Jackson, Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson,_Mississippi).[^cite_note-206] Many were jailed in the Mississippi State Penitentiary at Parchman. Mississippi employed the [trusty system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trusty_system_\(prison\)), a hierarchical order of inmates that used some inmates to control and enforce punishment of other inmates.[^cite_note-207]

In 1970, the civil rights lawyer Roy Haber began taking statements from inmates. He collected 50 pages of details of murders, rapes, beatings, and other abuses suffered by the inmates from 1969 to 1971 at the Mississippi State Penitentiary. In a [landmark case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmark_case) known as *[Gates v. Collier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gates_v._Collier)* (1972), four inmates represented by Haber sued the superintendent of Parchman Farm for violating their rights under the [United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution).

Federal Judge [William C. Keady](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_C._Keady) found in favor of the inmates, writing that Parchman Farm violated the civil rights of the inmates by inflicting [cruel and unusual punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruel_and_unusual_punishment). He ordered an immediate end to all unconstitutional conditions and practices. Racial segregation of inmates was abolished, as was the trusty system, which allowed certain inmates to have power and control over others.[^cite_note-hnet-208]

The prison was renovated in 1972 after the scathing ruling by Keady, who wrote that the prison was an affront to "modern standards of decency." Among other reforms, the accommodations were made habitable. The system of trusties was abolished. (The prison had armed [lifers](148681.md) with rifles and given them authority to oversee and guard other inmates, which led to many cases of abuse and murders.)[^cite_note-209]

In integrated correctional facilities in northern and western states, Black people represented a disproportionate number of prisoners, in excess of their proportion of the general population. They were often treated as second-class citizens by white correctional officers. Black people also represented a disproportionately high number of [death row](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_row) inmates. [Eldridge Cleaver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eldridge_Cleaver)'s book *[Soul on Ice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul_on_Ice_\(book\))* was written from his experiences in the California correctional system; it contributed to black militancy.[^cite_note-210]

### Legacy

Civil rights protest activity had an observable impact on white Americans' views on race and politics over time.[^cite_note-mazumdar-211] White people who live in counties in which civil rights protests of historical significance occurred have been found to have lower levels of racial resentment against Black people, are more likely to identify with the [Democratic Party](5043544.md), as well as more likely to support [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action).[^cite_note-mazumdar-211]

One study found that non-violent activism of the era tended to produce favorable media coverage and changes in public opinion, focusing on the issues organizers were raising, but violent protests tended to generate unfavorable media coverage that generated public desire to restore law and order.[^cite_note-212]

The 1964 Act was passed to end [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination) in various fields based on race, color, religion, sex, or national origin in employment and public accommodations.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] The 1964 Act did not prohibit sex discrimination against persons employed at educational institutions. A parallel law, Title VI, was also enacted in 1964 to prohibit discrimination in federally funded public and private entities. It covered race, color, and national origin but excluded sex. Feminists during the early 1970s lobbied Congress to add sex as a protected class category. In 1972, [Title IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_IX) was enacted to fill this gap and prohibit discrimination in all federally funded education programs. Title IX, or the [Education Amendments of 1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_Amendments_of_1972), was later renamed the *[Patsy T. Mink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patsy_T._Mink) Equal Opportunity in Education Act* following Mink's death in 2002.[^cite_note-215]

## Characteristics



[Fannie Lou Hamer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fannie_Lou_Hamer) of the [Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Freedom_Democratic_Party) (and other Mississippi-based organizations) is an example of local grassroots leadership in the movement.

### African-American women

Main article: [African-American women in the civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American_women_in_the_civil_rights_movement)

[African-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African-American) women in the civil rights movement were pivotal to its success.[^cite_note-gyant-216] They volunteered as activists, advocates, educators, clerics, writers, spiritual guides, caretakers, and politicians for the civil rights movement, leading and participating in organizations that contributed to the cause of civil rights.[^cite_note-gyant-216] [Rosa Parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosa_Parks)'s refusal to sit at the back of a [public bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_bus) resulted in the year-long [Montgomery bus boycott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montgomery_bus_boycott),[^cite_note-gyant-216] and the eventual [desegregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desegregation_in_the_United_States) of interstate travel in the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-217] Women were members of the NAACP because they believed it could help them contribute to the cause of civil rights.[^cite_note-gyant-216] Some of those involved with the Black Panthers were nationally recognized as leaders, and still others did editorial work on the *Black Panther* newspaper spurring internal discussions about gender issues.[^cite_note-Greene-218] [Ella Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Baker) founded the [SNCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Nonviolent_Coordinating_Committee) and was a prominent figure in the civil rights movement.[^cite_note-urban-219][^cite_note-220] Female students involved with the SNCC helped to organize sit-ins and the Freedom Rides.[^cite_note-urban-219] At the same time many elderly black women in towns across the Southern US cared for the organization's volunteers at their homes, providing the students food, a bed, healing aid and motherly love.[^cite_note-urban-219] Other women involved also formed church groups, bridge clubs, and professional organizations, such as the [National Council of Negro Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_of_Negro_Women), to help achieve freedom for themselves and their race.[^cite_note-Greene-218] Several who participated in these organizations lost their jobs because of their involvement.[^cite_note-Greene-218]

#### Sexist discrimination

Many women who participated in the movement experienced [gender discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexism) and [sexual harassment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_harassment).[^cite_note-221] There are many other accounts and examples throughout history.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-\:2-223][^cite_note-\:3-224][^cite_note-\:4-225] Although women formed the backbone of many civil rights movements from behind the scenes efforts (ex. grassroot campaigns, sustained community support, and recruiting), they were routinely excluded from formal leadership roles.[^cite_note-\:5-226] In the Southern Christian Leadership Conference (SCLC), for example, Ella Baker's strategic insights and organizing expertise were repeatedly undervalued, despite her being the most experienced staff member.[^cite_note-\:6-227] In a journal by Robnett (1996), she illustrates how Bakers experience was not an exception but a part of a greater pattern in which black women were confined to a term called "bridge leadership".[^cite_note-\:5-226] "Bridge leadership" is the term for someone who plays a crucial but undervalued role linking community members to formal structures of political movement. This hierarchy is often gendered, meaning male leaders received public recognition and authority, while women shouldered the bulk of the labor. Other significant accounts are documented similarly across the SNCC, CORE, and local NAACP branches, where women challenged sexist assumptions even as they fought racial injustice.[^cite_note-\:2-223][^cite_note-\:3-224][^cite_note-\:4-225][^cite_note-bostonreview\.net-228]

### Avoiding the "Communist" label

See also: [Communist Party USA and African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_USA_and_African_Americans)

On December 17, 1951, the [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_USA)–affiliated [Civil Rights Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Congress) delivered the petition *[We Charge Genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_Charge_Genocide): The Crime of Government Against the Negro People* to the United Nations, arguing that the U.S. federal government, by its failure to act against [lynching in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynching_in_the_United_States), was guilty of [genocide](12441.md) under Article II of the [UN Genocide Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_on_the_Prevention_and_Punishment_of_the_Crime_of_Genocide) (see [Black genocide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_genocide)).[^cite_note-229] The petition was presented to the United Nations at two separate venues: [Paul Robeson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Robeson), a concert singer and activist, presented it to a UN official in New York City, while [William L. Patterson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_L._Patterson), executive director of the CRC, delivered copies of the drafted petition to a UN delegation in Paris.[^cite_note-autogenerated1981-230]

Patterson, the editor of the petition, was a leader of the Communist Party USA and head of the [International Labor Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Labor_Defense), a group that offered legal representation to communists, trade unionists, and African Americans in cases involving political or racial persecution. The ILD was known for leading the defense of the [Scottsboro Boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottsboro_Boys) in [Alabama](303.md) in 1931, a time when the Communist Party had considerable influence among African Americans. This influence had largely declined by the late 1950s, although it could command international attention. As earlier civil rights figures such as Robeson, Du Bois, and Patterson became more politically radical (and therefore targets of Cold War [anti-Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Communism) by the U.S. Government), they lost favor with mainstream Black America as well as with the NAACP.[^cite_note-autogenerated1981-230]

To secure a place in the political mainstream and gain the broadest base of support, the new generation of civil rights activists believed it had to openly distance itself from anything and anyone associated with the Communist Party. According to [Ella Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Baker), the Southern Christian Leadership Conference added the word "Christian" to its name to deter charges that it was associated with [Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism).[^cite_note-\:6-227] Under [J. Edgar Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Edgar_Hoover), the FBI had been concerned about communism since the early 20th century, and it kept civil rights activists under close surveillance and labeled some of them "Communist" or "subversive", a practice that continued during the civil rights movement. In the early 1960s, the practice of distancing the civil rights movement from "Reds" was challenged by the [Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Nonviolent_Coordinating_Committee), which adopted a policy of accepting assistance and participation from anyone who supported the SNCC's political program and was willing to "put their body on the line, regardless of political affiliation." At times, the SNCC's policy of political openness put it at odds with the NAACP.[^cite_note-autogenerated1981-230]

### Grassroots leadership

While most popular representations of the movement center on the leadership and philosophy of Martin Luther King Jr., some scholars note that the movement was too diverse to be attributed to a single person, organization, or strategy. Sociologist [Doug McAdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_McAdam) has stated that, "in King's case, it would be inaccurate to say that he was the leader of the modern civil rights movement...but more importantly, there was no singular civil rights movement. The movement was, in fact, a coalition of thousands of local efforts nationwide, spanning several decades, hundreds of discrete groups, and all manner of strategies and tactics—legal, illegal, institutional, non-institutional, violent, non-violent. Without discounting King's importance, it would be sheer fiction to call him the leader of what was fundamentally an amorphous, fluid, dispersed movement."[^cite_note-bostonreview\.net-228] Decentralized grassroots leadership has been a major focus of movement scholarship in recent decades through the work of historians [John Dittmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Dittmer), [Charles Payne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_M._Payne), [Barbara Ransby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Ransby), and others.

### Tactics and nonviolence



Armed Lumbee Indians aggressively confronting Klansmen in the [Battle of Hayes Pond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hayes_Pond)

The Jim Crow system employed "terror as a means of social control,"[^cite_note-231] with the most organized manifestations being the [Ku Klux Klan](16779.md) and their collaborators in local police departments. This violence played a key role in blocking the civil rights movement's progress in the late 1950s. Some black organizations in the South began practicing armed self-defense. The first to do so openly was the Monroe, North Carolina, chapter of the [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP) led by [Robert F. Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Williams). Williams had rebuilt the chapter after its membership was terrorized out of public life by the Klan. He did so by encouraging a new, more working-class membership to arm itself thoroughly and defend against attack.[^cite_note-232] When Klan nightriders attacked the home of NAACP member Albert Perry in October 1957, Williams' militia exchanged gunfire with the stunned Klansmen, who quickly retreated. The following day, the city council held an emergency session and passed an ordinance banning KKK motorcades.[^cite_note-233] One year later, Lumbee Indians in North Carolina would have a similarly successful armed stand-off with the Klan (known as the [Battle of Hayes Pond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Hayes_Pond)), which resulted in KKK leader James W. "Catfish" Cole being convicted of incitement to riot.[^cite_note-234]

After the acquittal of several white men charged with sexually assaulting black women in Monroe, Williams announced to United Press International reporters that he would "meet violence with violence" as a policy. Williams' declaration was quoted on the front page of *The New York Times*, and *The Carolina Times* considered it "the biggest civil rights story of 1959".[^cite_note-235] NAACP National chairman Roy Wilkins immediately suspended Williams from his position, but the Monroe organizer won support from numerous NAACP chapters across the country. Ultimately, Wilkins resorted to bribing influential organizer Daisy Bates to campaign against Williams at the NAACP national convention, and the suspension was upheld. The convention nonetheless passed a resolution which stated: "We do not deny, but reaffirm the right of individual and collective self-defense against unlawful assaults."[^cite_note-236] Martin Luther King Jr. argued for Williams' removal,[^cite_note-237] but [Ella Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Baker)[^cite_note-238] and [WEB Dubois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._E._B._Du_Bois)[^cite_note-history.msu.edu-239] both publicly praised the Monroe leader's position.

Williams—along with his wife, Mabel Williams—continued to play a leadership role in the Monroe movement, and to some degree, in the national movement. The Williamses published *The Crusader*, a nationally circulated newsletter, beginning in 1960, and the influential book *Negroes With Guns* in 1962. Williams did not call for full militarization in this period, but "flexibility in the freedom struggle."[^cite_note-240] Williams was well-versed in legal tactics and publicity, which he had used successfully in the internationally known "[Kissing Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kissing_Case)" of 1958, as well as nonviolent methods, which he used at [lunch counter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunch_counter) sit-ins in Monroe—all with armed self-defense as a complementary tactic.

Williams led the Monroe movement in another armed stand-off with white supremacists during an August 1961 Freedom Ride; he had been invited to participate in the campaign by [Ella Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Baker) and [James Forman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Forman) of the Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee (SNCC). The incident (along with his campaigns for peace with Cuba) resulted in him being targeted by the FBI and prosecuted for kidnapping; he was cleared of all charges in 1976.[^cite_note-Tyson_1998-241] Meanwhile, armed self-defense continued discreetly in the Southern movement with such figures as SNCC's [Amzie Moore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amzie_Moore),[^cite_note-Tyson_1998-241] [Hartman Turnbow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hartman_Turnbow),[^cite_note-242] and [Fannie Lou Hamer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fannie_Lou_Hamer)[^cite_note-243] all willing to use arms to defend their lives from nightrides. Taking refuge from the FBI in Cuba, the Willamses broadcast the radio show *[Radio Free Dixie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Free_Dixie)* throughout the eastern United States via Radio Progresso beginning in 1962.
