In this period, Williams advocated guerrilla warfare against racist institutions and saw the large ghetto riots of the era as a manifestation of his strategy.

[University of North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_North_Carolina_at_Chapel_Hill) historian Walter Rucker has written that "the emergence of Robert F Williams contributed to the marked decline in anti-black racial violence in the U.S....After centuries of anti-black violence, African Americans across the country began to defend their communities aggressively—employing overt force when necessary. This in turn evoked in whites real fear of black vengeance..." This opened up space for African Americans to use nonviolent demonstrations with less fear of deadly reprisal.[^cite_note-244] Of the many civil rights activists who share this view, the most prominent was Rosa Parks. Parks gave the eulogy at Williams' funeral in 1996, praising him for "his courage and for his commitment to freedom," and concluding that "The sacrifices he made, and what he did, should go down in history and never be forgotten."[^cite_note-245]

### Jewish support for the movement

Jewish Americans played an active role in supporting the civil rights movement. They were actively involved in establishing and supporting a number of the most important civil rights organizations, including the NAACP, the Leadership Conference on Civil and Human Rights, the Southern Christian Leadership Conference (SCLC), and the Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee (SNCC). These organizations played pivotal roles in the civil rights movement, advocating for racial equality and justice.[^cite_note-246]

Despite representing less than 2% of the US population, Jewish people made up roughly half of all civil rights lawyers in the South during the 1960s and half of the white northern volunteers involved in the 1964 Mississippi Freedom Summer project.[^cite_note-247]

### Opposition to police brutality

Organizers from the [Congress of Racial Equality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Racial_Equality) and the [Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_Nonviolent_Coordinating_Committee) confronted police violence with sit-ins at precinct stations, picketing outside department headquarters, and blocking traffic to draw attention to officer misdeeds. In return, activists found themselves the targets of political repression in the form of pervasive police surveillance, infiltration by undercover officers, and retaliatory prosecutions aimed at discrediting their movement. Many civil rights leaders—including [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md), [Ella Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ella_Baker), [James Forman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Forman), [Fannie Lou Hamer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fannie_Lou_Hamer), and [John Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lewis)—criticized police brutality in writing and speeches at various points.[^cite_note-248]

## Political responses

### Truman administration: 1945–1953

Partly in response to the [March on Washington Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_on_Washington_Movement) under Truman's predecessor, Franklin D. Roosevelt, the [Fair Employment Practices Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fair_Employment_Practices_Committee) was created to address racial discrimination in employment,[^cite_note-249] and in 1946, Truman created the [President's Committee on Civil Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President's_Committee_on_Civil_Rights). On June 29, 1947, Truman became the first president to address the demands of the [National Association for the Advancement of Colored People](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_for_the_Advancement_of_Colored_People) (NAACP). The speech took place at the [Lincoln Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial) during the NAACP convention and was carried nationally on the radio. In that speech, Truman laid out his agreement with the need to end discrimination, which he would advance through the first comprehensive, presidentially proposed civil rights legislation. Truman on "civil rights and human freedom" declared:[^cite_note-250]

> … Our immediate task is to remove the last remnants of the barriers which stand between millions of our citizens and their birthright. There is no justifiable reason for discrimination because of ancestry, or religion, or race, or color. We must not tolerate such limitations on the freedom of any of our people and on their enjoyment of basic rights which every citizen in a truly democratic society must possess.

In February 1948, Truman delivered a formal message to Congress requesting adoption of his 10-point program to secure civil rights, including anti-lynching, voter rights, and the elimination of segregation. "No political act since the [Compromise of 1877](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compromise_of_1877)," argued biographer [Taylor Branch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taylor_Branch), "so profoundly influenced race relations; in a sense it was a repeal of 1877."[^cite_note-Mikas-251] Truman was opposed by the [conservative coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_coalition) in congress, so instead issued Executive Orders 9980 and 9981 ending discrimination in federal employment and in the armed forces.[^cite_note-Mikas-251]

### Eisenhower administration: 1953–1961

While not a key focus of his administration, President Eisenhower made several conservative strides toward making America a racially integrated country. The year he was elected, Eisenhower desegregated Washington D.C. after hearing a story about an African American man who was unable to rent a hotel room, buy a meal, access drinking water, and attend a movie.[^cite_note-auto-252] Shortly after this act, Eisenhower utilized Hollywood personalities to pressure movie theatres into desegregating as well.[^cite_note-253]

Under the previous administration, President Truman signed [Executive Order 9981 to desegregate the military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_9981). However, Truman's executive order had hardly been enforced. President Eisenhower made it a point to enforce the executive order. By October 30, 1954, there were no segregated combat units in the United States.[^cite_note-auto-252] Not only this, but Eisenhower also desegregated the Veterans Administration and military bases in the South, including federal schools for military dependents. Expanding his work beyond the military, Eisenhower formed two non-discrimination committees, one to broker nondiscrimination agreements with government contractors and a second to end discrimination within government departments and agencies.[^cite_note-auto-252]

The first major piece of civil rights legislation since the Civil Rights Act of 1875 was also passed under the Eisenhower administration. President Eisenhower proposed, championed, and signed the [Civil Rights Act of 1957](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1957). The legislation established the Civil Rights Commission and the Justice Department's Civil Rights Division, and banned intimidation, coercion, and other means of interfering with a citizen's right to vote. Eisenhower's work in desegregating the judicial system is also notable. The judges he appointed were liberal when it came to the subject of civil rights/desegregation, and he actively avoided placing segregationists in federal courts.[^cite_note-auto-252]

### Kennedy administration: 1961–1963



[Attorney General](44002.md) [Robert F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy) speaking before a hostile civil rights crowd protesting low [minority hiring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action_in_the_United_States) in his [Justice Department](52563.md), June 14, 1963

[^cite_note-Risen_2014_76-254]

For the first two years of the Kennedy administration, civil rights activists had mixed opinions of both the president and his younger brother, [Robert F. Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy), the [Attorney General](44002.md). Historian [David Halberstam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Halberstam) wrote that the race question was for a long time a minor ethnic political issue in [Massachusetts](1645518.md), where the Kennedy brothers came from, and had they been from another part of the country, "they might have been more immediately sensitive to the complexities and depth of black feelings."[^cite_note-255] A well of historical skepticism toward liberal politics had left African Americans with a sense of uneasy disdain for any white politician who claimed to share their concerns for freedom, particularly ones connected to the historically pro-segregationist Democratic Party. Still, many were encouraged by the discreet support Kennedy gave to King and the administration's willingness, after dramatic pressure from civil disobedience, to introduce racially egalitarian initiatives.

Robert F. Kennedy expressed the administration's commitment to civil rights during a May 6, 1961 [speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_Day_Address) at the [University of Georgia Law School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Georgia_Law_School):

> Our position is quite clear. We are upholding the law. The federal government would not be running the schools in [Prince Edward County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massive_resistance) any more than it is running the University of Georgia or the schools in my home state of [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boston_desegregation_busing_crisis#Racial_Imbalance_Act). In this case, in all cases, I say to you today that if the orders of the court are circumvented, the Department of Justice will act. We will not stand by or be aloof—we will move. I happen to believe that [the 1954 decision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_v._Board_of_Education) was right. But my belief does not matter. It is now the law. Some of you may believe the decision was wrong. That does not matter. It is the law.[^cite_note-256]

Many of the initiatives resulted from Robert Kennedy's passion. The younger Kennedy gained a rapid education in the realities of racism through events such as the [Baldwin–Kennedy meeting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baldwin%E2%80%93Kennedy_meeting). The president came to share his brother's sense of urgency on the matter, resulting in the landmark [Civil Rights Address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Address) of June 1963 and the introduction of the first major civil rights act of the decade.[^cite_note-257][^cite_note-258]

That same month, during the [Freedom Rides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Rides), Robert Kennedy became concerned with the issue when photographs of the burning bus and savage beatings in [Anniston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anniston,_Alabama) and Birmingham were broadcast around the world. They came at an especially embarrassing time, as President Kennedy was about to have a [summit with the Soviet premier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vienna_summit) in Vienna. The White House was concerned with its image among the populations of newly independent nations in Africa and Asia, and Robert Kennedy responded with an address for [Voice of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voice_of_America) stating that great progress had been made on the issue of race relations. Meanwhile, behind the scenes, the administration worked to resolve the crisis with minimal violence and to prevent the Freedom Riders from generating a fresh crop of headlines that might divert attention from the President's international agenda. The [*Freedom Riders*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Riders_\(film\)) documentary notes that, "The back burner issue of civil rights had collided with the urgent demands of Cold War [realpolitik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Realpolitik)."[^cite_note-259]

On May 21, when a white mob attacked and burned the First Baptist Church in Montgomery, Alabama, where King was holding out with protesters, Robert Kennedy telephoned King to ask him to stay in the building until the U.S. Marshals and National Guard could secure the area. King proceeded to berate Kennedy for "allowing the situation to continue". King later publicly thanked Kennedy for deploying the force to break up an attack that might otherwise have ended King's life.

With a very small majority in Congress, the president's ability to press ahead with legislation relied considerably on a balancing game with the Senators and Congressmen of the South. Without the support of Vice President Johnson, a former Senator with years of experience in Congress and longstanding ties there, many of the Attorney-General's programs would not have progressed.

By late 1962, frustration at the slow pace of political change was balanced by the movement's strong support for legislative initiatives, including administrative representation across all U.S. Government departments and greater access to the ballot box. From squaring off against Governor [George Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Wallace), to "tearing into" Vice President Johnson (for failing to desegregate areas of the administration), to threatening corrupt white Southern judges with disbarment, to desegregating interstate transport, Robert Kennedy came to be consumed by the civil rights movement. He continued to work on these social justice issues in his 1968 bid for the presidency.

On the night of Governor Wallace's capitulation to African-American enrollment at the [University of Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Alabama), President Kennedy gave an [address](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Address) to the nation, which marked the changing tide. This address would become a landmark in the ensuing shift in political policy regarding civil rights. In 1966, Robert Kennedy visited South Africa and voiced his objections to [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid), the first time a major US politician had done so:

> At the [University of Natal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Natal) in Durban, I was told the church to which most of the white population belongs teaches apartheid as a moral necessity. A questioner declared that few churches allow black Africans to pray with the white because the Bible says that is the way it should be, because God created Negroes to serve. "But suppose God is black", I replied. "What if we go to Heaven and we, all our lives, have treated the Negro as an inferior, and God is there, and we look up and He is not white? What then is our response?" There was no answer. Only silence.

— *LOOK* Magazine[^cite_note-260]

Robert Kennedy's relationship with the movement was not always positive. As attorney general, he was called to account by activists—who booed him at a June 1963 speech—for the Justice Department's own poor record of hiring Black people.[^cite_note-Risen_2014_76-254] He also presided over [FBI Director](5861065.md) [J. Edgar Hoover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J._Edgar_Hoover) and his [COINTELPRO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COINTELPRO) program. This program ordered FBI agents to "expose, disrupt, misdirect, discredit, or otherwise neutralize" the activities of Communist front groups, a category in which the paranoid Hoover included most civil rights organizations.[^cite_note-WRH-261][^cite_note-pbsco-262] Kennedy personally authorized some of the programs.[^cite_note-263] According to [Tim Weiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Weiner), "RFK knew much more about this surveillance than he ever admitted." Although Kennedy only approved the limited wiretapping of King's phones "on a trial basis, for a month or so." Hoover extended the clearance so his men were "unshackled" to look for evidence in any areas of the black leader's life they deemed important; they then used this information to harass King.[^cite_note-264] Kennedy directly ordered surveillance on [James Baldwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Baldwin) after their antagonistic racial summit in 1963.[^cite_note-265][^cite_note-266]

### Johnson administration: 1963–1969

Further information: [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964), [War on Poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_Poverty), and [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson)



President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) signs the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965)

Lyndon Johnson made civil rights one of his highest priorities, coupling it with his [War on Poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/War_on_Poverty). However, the increasing opposition to the Vietnam War, coupled with the cost of the war, undercut support for his domestic programs.[^cite_note-267]

Under Kennedy, major civil rights legislation had been stalled in Congress. His assassination changed everything. On one hand, President Lyndon Johnson was a much more skillful negotiator than Kennedy, but he had behind him a powerful national momentum demanding immediate action on moral and emotional grounds. Demands for immediate action originated from unexpected directions, especially white Protestant church groups. The Justice Department, led by Robert Kennedy, moved from a posture of defending him against the quagmire of racial politics to one of fulfilling his legacy. The violent death and public reaction dramatically moved the conservative Republicans, led by Senator [Everett McKinley Dirksen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everett_McKinley_Dirksen), whose support was the margin of victory for the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964). The act immediately ended de jure (legal) segregation and the era of Jim Crow.[^cite_note-268]

With the civil rights movement at full blast, Lyndon Johnson coupled black entrepreneurship with his war on poverty, establishing special programs within the Small Business Administration, the Office of Economic Opportunity, and other agencies.[^cite_note-269] This time, there was money for loans designed to boost minority business ownership. Richard Nixon greatly expanded the program, setting up the Office of Minority Business Enterprise (OMBE) in the expectation that black entrepreneurs would help defuse racial tensions and possibly support his reelection.[^cite_note-270]

### Foreign political reactions

#### China

In China, [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong) in August 1963 expressed support for the U.S. civil rights movement, stating that the "fascist atrocities" committed against black people in the U.S. demonstrated the link between reactionary domestic U.S. policies and its policies of aggression abroad.[^cite_note-\:Minami-271]<sup>: 34 </sup> In 1968, a mass rally in China condemned the assassination of Martin Luther King Jr.[^cite_note-\:Reinders-272]<sup>: 91 </sup> Mao stated that racial discrimination in the U.S. resulted from its colonial system and that the struggle of Black people in the U.S. was an [anti-imperialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-imperialism) struggle.[^cite_note-\:Minami-271]<sup>: 34 </sup> The [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) echoed this view of the civil rights movement.[^cite_note-\:Reinders-272]<sup>: 91 </sup> During the [Cultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_Revolution), the *[People's Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Daily)* repeated cited the example that King advocated nonviolence, but was violently killed, as an example of its view that violent struggle was necessary for the oppressed masses of the world to free themselves.[^cite_note-\:Gao-273]

[Maoism](46636.md) influenced some components of the Black liberation movement, including the Black Panther Party and black self-defense advocate [Robert F. Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Williams).[^cite_note-\:Minami-271]<sup>: 34 </sup>

#### Soviet Union

There was an international context to the U.S. federal government's actions during these years. The Soviet media frequently covered [racial discrimination in the U.S.](2162835.md)[^cite_note-274] Deeming American criticism of [its own human rights abuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_the_Soviet_Union) hypocritical, the Soviet government would respond by stating "[And you are lynching Negroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/And_you_are_lynching_Negroes)".[^cite_note-275] In his 1934 book *Russia Today: What Can We Learn from It?*, [Sherwood Eddy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherwood_Eddy) wrote: "In the most remote villages of Russia today Americans are frequently asked what they are going to do to the [Scottsboro Negro boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scottsboro_boys) and why they lynch Negroes."[^cite_note-sherwoodeddy-276]

In *Cold War Civil Rights: Race and the Image of American Democracy*, the historian [Mary L. Dudziak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_L._Dudziak) wrote that Communists who were critical of the United States accused it of practicing hypocrisy when it portrayed itself as the "leader of the free world," while so many of its citizens were being subjected to severe racial discrimination and violence; she argued that this was a major factor in moving the government to support civil rights legislation.[^cite_note-277]

## Popular reactions

### Malcolm X's relationship with the movement, 1964–1965

Main articles: [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X), [Black Nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Nationalism), and [The Ballot or the Bullet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ballot_or_the_Bullet)

In March 1964, [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X) (el-Hajj Malik el-Shabazz), national representative of the [Nation of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_of_Islam), formally broke with that organization, and made a public offer to collaborate with any civil rights organization that accepted the right to self-defense and the philosophy of Black nationalism (which Malcolm said no longer required [Black separatism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_separatism)). [Gloria Richardson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gloria_Richardson), head of the [Cambridge, Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge,_Maryland), chapter of [SNCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SNCC), and leader of the Cambridge rebellion,[^cite_note-278] an honored guest at The March on Washington, immediately embraced Malcolm's offer. Mrs. Richardson, "the nation's most prominent woman \[civil rights] leader,"[^cite_note-BAA-279] told *[The Baltimore Afro-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Baltimore_Afro-American)* that "Malcolm is being very practical. ... The federal government has moved into conflict situations only when matters approach the level of insurrection. Self-defense may force Washington to intervene sooner."[^cite_note-BAA-279] Earlier, in May 1963, writer and activist [James Baldwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Baldwin) had stated publicly that "the Black Muslim movement is the only one in the country we can call [grassroots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassroots), I hate to say it. ... Malcolm articulates for Negroes, their suffering ... he corroborates their reality."[^cite_note-280] On the local level, Malcolm and the NOI had been allied with the Harlem chapter of the Congress of Racial Equality (CORE) since at least 1962.[^cite_note-281]

Malcolm X and Martin Luther King Jr. speak to each other thoughtfully as others look on.

[Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X) meets with [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md), March 26, 1964

On March 26, 1964, as the Civil Rights Act faced stiff opposition in Congress, Malcolm held a public meeting with Martin Luther King Jr. at the Capitol. Malcolm had tried to begin a dialogue with King as early as 1957, but King had rebuffed him. Malcolm had responded by calling King an "[Uncle Tom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncle_Tom)", saying he had turned his back on black militancy to appease the white power structure. But the two men were on good terms at their face-to-face meeting.[^cite_note-282] There is evidence that King was preparing to support Malcolm's plan to formally bring the U.S. government before the United Nations on charges of human rights violations against African Americans.[^cite_note-283] Malcolm now encouraged Black nationalists to get involved in voter registration drives and other forms of community organizing to redefine and expand the movement.[^cite_note-284]

Civil rights activists became increasingly combative in the 1963 to 1964 period, seeking to defy such events as the thwarting of the Albany campaign, police repression, and [Ku Klux Klan terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_Street_Baptist_Church_bombing) in [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham_campaign), and the assassination of [Medgar Evers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medgar_Evers). The latter's brother Charles Evers, who took over as Mississippi NAACP Field Director, told a public NAACP conference on February 15, 1964, that "non-violence won't work in Mississippi ... we made up our minds ... that if a white man shoots at a Negro in Mississippi, we will shoot back."[^cite_note-285] The repression of sit-ins in [Jacksonville, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonville,_Florida), provoked a riot in which black youth threw [Molotov cocktails](20922.md) at police on March 24, 1964.[^cite_note-286] Malcolm X gave numerous speeches in this period warning that such militant activity would escalate further if African Americans' rights were not fully recognized. In his landmark April 1964 speech "[The Ballot or the Bullet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ballot_or_the_Bullet)", Malcolm presented an ultimatum to white America: "There's new strategy coming in. It'll be Molotov cocktails this month, hand grenades next month, and something else next month. It'll be ballots, or it'll be bullets."[^cite_note-287]

As noted in the PBS documentary *[Eyes on the Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eyes_on_the_Prize)*, "Malcolm X had a far-reaching effect on the civil rights movement. In the South, there had been a long tradition of self-reliance. Malcolm X's ideas now touched that tradition".[^cite_note-288] Self-reliance was becoming paramount in light of the [1964 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_Democratic_National_Convention)'s decision to refuse seating to the [Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mississippi_Freedom_Democratic_Party) (MFDP) and instead to seat the regular state delegation, which had been elected in violation of the party's own rules, and by [Jim Crow law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow_law) instead.[^cite_note-289] SNCC moved in an increasingly militant direction and worked with Malcolm X on two Harlem MFDP fundraisers in December 1964.

When [Fannie Lou Hamer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fannie_Lou_Hamer) spoke to Harlemites about the Jim Crow violence that she'd suffered in Mississippi, she linked it directly to the Northern police brutality against Black people that Malcolm protested against;[^cite_note-290] When Malcolm asserted that African Americans should emulate the [Mau Mau army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenya_Land_and_Freedom_Army) of [Kenya](188171.md) in efforts to gain their independence, many in SNCC applauded.[^cite_note-291]

During the [Selma campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selma_to_Montgomery_marches) for voting rights in 1965, Malcolm made it known that he'd heard reports of increased threats of lynching around Selma. In late January he sent an open telegram to [George Lincoln Rockwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Lincoln_Rockwell), the head of the [American Nazi Party](364176.md), stating:

> "if your present racist agitation against our people there in Alabama causes physical harm to Reverend King or any other black Americans ... you and your KKK friends will be met with maximum physical retaliation from those of us who are not handcuffed by the disarming philosophy of nonviolence."[^cite_note-292]

The following month, the Selma chapter of SNCC invited Malcolm to speak to a mass meeting there. On the day of Malcolm's appearance, President Johnson made his first public statement in support of the Selma campaign.[^cite_note-293] Paul Ryan Haygood, a co-director of the [NAACP Legal Defense Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP_Legal_Defense_Fund), credits Malcolm with a role in gaining support from the federal government. Haygood noted that "shortly after Malcolm's visit to Selma, a federal judge, responding to a suit brought by the [Department of Justice](52563.md), required [Dallas County, Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dallas_County,_Alabama), registrars to process at least 100 Black applications each day their offices were open."[^cite_note-294]

### American Jewish People

See also: [African American–Jewish relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American%E2%80%93Jewish_relations); [New York City teachers' strike of 1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_teachers'_strike_of_1968); and [Brownsville, Brooklyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brownsville,_Brooklyn)

Main article: [Jews in the civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jews_in_the_civil_rights_movement)



Jewish civil rights activist [Joseph L. Rauh Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_L._Rauh_Jr.) marching with [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md) in 1963

Many in the [Jewish](1131183.md) community supported the civil rights movement. In fact, statistically, Jewish people were one of the most actively involved non-black groups in the Movement. Many Jewish students worked in concert with African Americans for CORE, SCLC, and SNCC as full-time organizers and summer volunteers during the Civil Rights era. Jewish people made up roughly half of the white northern and western volunteers involved in the 1964 Mississippi [Freedom Summer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Summer) project and approximately half of the civil rights attorneys active in the South during the 1960s.[^cite_note-295]

Jewish leaders were arrested while heeding a call from Martin Luther King Jr. in [St. Augustine, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Augustine,_Florida), in June 1964, where the largest mass arrest of rabbis in American history took place at the Monson Motor Lodge. [Abraham Joshua Heschel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Joshua_Heschel), a writer, rabbi, and professor of theology at the [Jewish Theological Seminary of America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_Theological_Seminary_of_America) in New York, was outspoken on the subject of civil rights. He marched arm-in-arm with King in the 1965 [Selma to Montgomery march](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selma_to_Montgomery_marches#The_march_to_Montgomery). In the 1964 [murders of Chaney, Goodman, and Schwerner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murders_of_Chaney,_Goodman,_and_Schwerner), the two white activists killed, [Andrew Goodman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Goodman_\(activist\)) and [Michael Schwerner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Schwerner), were both Jewish.

[Brandeis University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brandeis_University), the only nonsectarian Jewish-sponsored college university in the world, created the Transitional Year Program (TYP) in 1968, in part response to the [assassination of Martin Luther King Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Martin_Luther_King_Jr.) The faculty created it to renew the university's commitment to social justice. Recognizing Brandeis as a university committed to academic excellence, these faculty members created an opportunity for disadvantaged students to participate in an empowering educational experience.

The [American Jewish Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Jewish_Committee), [American Jewish Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Jewish_Congress), and [Anti-Defamation League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Defamation_League) (ADL) actively promoted civil rights. While Jewish people were very active in the civil rights movement in the South, in the North, many had experienced a more strained relationship with African Americans.  It has been argued that with Black militancy and the [Black Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Power) movements on the rise, "Black antisemitism" increased, leading to strained relations between Black people and Jewish people in Northern communities. In New York City, most notably, there was a major socio-economic class difference in the perception of African Americans by Jewish people.[^cite_note-296] Jewish people from better-educated upper-middle-class backgrounds were often very supportive of African American civil rights activities. In contrast, the Jewish people in poorer urban communities that became increasingly minority were often less supportive, largely due to more negative and violent interactions between the two groups.

According to political scientist [Michael Rogin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Rogin), Jewish-Black hostility was a two-way street extending to earlier decades. In the post-World War II era, Jewish people were granted [white privilege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_privilege), and most moved into the middle-class, while Black people were left behind in the ghetto.[^cite_note-297] Urban Jewish people engaged in the same sort of conflicts with Black people—over [integration busing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integration_busing), local control of schools, housing, crime, communal identity, and class divides—that other [white ethnics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_ethnics) did, leading to Jewish people participating in [white flight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_flight). The culmination of this was the [1968 New York City teachers' strike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_teachers'_strike_of_1968), pitting largely Jewish schoolteachers against predominantly Black parents in [Brownsville, New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brownsville,_New_York).[^cite_note-298]

Due to the prohibition against discrimination based on race, nationality, and religion, the Fair Housing Act of 1968 prohibited both antisemitic and anti-Black [covenants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covenant_\(law\)) from being enforced. The act was supported by many Jewish organizations.[^cite_note-299]

#### Public profile

Many Jewish people in the Southern states who supported civil rights for African Americans tended to keep a low profile on "the race issue", to avoid attracting the attention of the anti-Black and antisemitic Ku Klux Klan.[^cite_note-My_Jewish_Learning-300] However, Klan groups exploited the issue of African-American integration and Jewish involvement in the struggle to commit violently antisemitic [hate crimes](13547.md). As an example of this hatred, in one year alone, from November 1957 to October 1958, temples and other Jewish communal gatherings were bombed and desecrated in [Atlanta](3138.md), [Nashville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nashville), [Jacksonville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacksonville,_Florida), and [Miami](53846.md), and [dynamite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynamite) was found under [synagogues](48559.md) in [Birmingham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birmingham,_Alabama), [Charlotte](57447.md), and [Gastonia, North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastonia,_North_Carolina). Some [rabbis](51273.md) received [death threats](13262036.md), but there were no injuries following these outbursts of [violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violence).[^cite_note-My_Jewish_Learning-300]

### Black segregationists

Despite the common notion that the ideas of [Martin Luther King Jr.](20076.md), [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X), and [Black Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Power) were in conflict with each other and constituted the only ideologies of the civil rights movement, many Black people held other views. Fearing that events during the movement were unfolding too quickly, some Black people felt that leaders should pursue their activism at an incremental pace. Others had reservations on how focused Black people were on the movement and felt that such attention was better spent on reforming issues within the black community.

While Conservatives, in general, supported integration, some defended incrementally phased-out segregation as a backstop against assimilation. Based on her interpretation of a 1966 study made by Donald Matthews and James Prothro detailing the relative percentage of Black people for integration, against it, or feeling something else, Lauren Winner asserts that:

> Black defenders of segregation look, at first blush, very much like black nationalists, especially in their preference for all-black institutions; but black defenders of segregation differ from nationalists in two key ways. First, while both groups criticize [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NAACP)-style integration, nationalists articulate a third alternative to integration and [Jim Crow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Crow), while segregationists preferred to stick with the status quo. Second, absent from black defenders of segregation's political vocabulary was the demand for [self-determination](29269.md). They called for all-black institutions, but not autonomous all-black institutions; indeed, some defenders of segregation asserted that black people needed white paternalism and oversight in order to thrive.[^cite_note-301]

Oftentimes, African-American community leaders would be staunch defenders of segregation. Church ministers, business people, and educators were among those who wished to keep segregation and segregationist ideals to retain the privileges they gained from patronage from whites, such as monetary gains. In addition, they relied on segregation to keep their jobs and economies in their communities thriving. It was feared that if integration became widespread in the South, black-owned businesses and other establishments would lose a large chunk of their customer base to white-owned businesses, and many Black people would lose opportunities for jobs that were presently exclusive to their interests.[^cite_note-302] On the other hand, there were the everyday, average black people who criticized integration as well. They took issue with various aspects of the civil rights movement and with the potential for Black people to exercise consumerism and economic liberty without hindrance from white people.[^cite_note-303]

For Martin Luther King Jr., Malcolm X, and other leading activists and groups during the movement, these opposing viewpoints posed obstacles to their ideas. These different views made these leaders' work much harder, but they were nonetheless important to the movement's overall scope. For the most part, the Black individuals who had reservations about various aspects of the movement and the activists' ideologies were not able to make a game-changing dent in the activists' efforts. Still, the existence of these alternative ideas gave some Black people an outlet to express their concerns about the changing social structure.

### "Black Power" militants

Main articles: [Black Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Power) and [Black Power movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Power_movement)



Gold medalist [Tommie Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommie_Smith) *(center)* and bronze medalist [John Carlos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Carlos) *(right)* showing the [raised fist on the podium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Olympics_Black_Power_salute) after the 200 m race at the [1968 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Summer_Olympics); both wear [Olympic Project for Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Project_for_Human_Rights) badges. [Peter Norman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Norman) *(silver medalist, left)* from Australia also wears an OPHR badge in solidarity with Smith and Carlos.

During the Freedom Summer campaign of 1964, numerous tensions within the civil rights movement came to the forefront. Many Black people in [SNCC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SNCC) developed concerns that white activists from the North and West were taking over the movement. The participation by numerous white students did not reduce the amount of violence that SNCC suffered but seemed to exacerbate it. Additionally, there was profound disillusionment at Lyndon Johnson's denial of voting status for the Mississippi Freedom Democratic Party at the Democratic National Convention.[^cite_note-304][^cite_note-305] Meanwhile, during [CORE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Racial_Equality)'s work in Louisiana that summer, that group found the federal government would not respond to requests to enforce the provisions of the Civil Rights Act of 1964, or to protect the lives of activists who challenged segregation. The Louisiana campaign survived by relying on a local African-American militia called the [Deacons for Defense and Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deacons_for_Defense_and_Justice), who used arms to repel white supremacist violence and police repression. CORE's collaboration with the Deacons was effective in disrupting Jim Crow in numerous Louisiana areas.[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307]

In 1965, SNCC helped organize an independent political party, the [Lowndes County Freedom Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lowndes_County_Freedom_Organization) (LCFO), in the heart of the Alabama Black Belt, also Klan territory. It permitted its black leaders to openly promote the use of armed self-defense. Meanwhile, the Deacons for Defense and Justice expanded into Mississippi and assisted [Charles Evers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Evers)'s NAACP chapter with a successful campaign in [Natchez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natchez,_Mississippi). Charles had taken the lead after his brother [Medgar Evers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medgar_Evers) was assassinated in 1963.[^cite_note-308] The same year, the 1965 [Watts Rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watts_Rebellion) took place in Los Angeles. Many black youths were committed to the use of violence to protest inequality and oppression.[^cite_note-309]

During the [March Against Fear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_Against_Fear) in 1966, initiated by [James Meredith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Meredith), SNCC and CORE fully embraced the slogan of "black power" to describe these trends towards militancy and self-reliance. In Mississippi, Stokely Carmichael declared, "I'm not going to beg the white man for anything that I deserve, I'm going to take it. We need power."[^cite_note-310]

Some people engaging in the Black Power movement claimed a growing sense of black pride and identity. In gaining more of a sense of a cultural identity, Black people demanded that whites no longer refer to them as "Negroes" but as "Afro-Americans," similar to other ethnic groups, such as Irish Americans and Italian Americans. Until the mid-1960s, Black people had dressed similarly to whites and often [straightened their hair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hair_straightening). As part of affirming their identity, Black people began wearing African-based [dashikis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dashiki) and growing their hair out in natural [afros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro). The afro, sometimes nicknamed the "'fro," remained a popular black hairstyle until the late 1970s. Other variations of traditional African styles have become popular, often featuring braids, extensions, and dreadlocks.

The [Black Panther Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Panther_Party) (BPP), founded by [Huey Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huey_Newton) and [Bobby Seale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobby_Seale) in [Oakland, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oakland,_California) in 1966, gained the most national attention for its Black Power activism. The group began following the revolutionary pan-Africanism of late-period [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X), using a "by-any-means necessary" approach to stopping racial inequality. They sought to rid African-American neighborhoods of [police brutality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality) and to establish [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist) [community control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_power) in the ghettos. While they conducted armed confrontations with the police, they also set up free breakfast and healthcare programs for children.[^cite_note-311] Between 1968 and 1971, the BPP was one of the most important black organizations in the country and had support from the NAACP, SCLC, [Peace and Freedom Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_and_Freedom_Party), and others.[^cite_note-312]

Black Power was taken to another level inside prison walls. In 1966, [George Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Jackson_\(Black_Panther\)) formed the [Black Guerrilla Family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Guerrilla_Family) in the California [San Quentin State Prison](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Quentin_State_Prison). The goal of this group was to overthrow the white-run government in America and the prison system. In 1970, this group displayed their dedication after a white correctional officer was found not guilty of shooting and killing three black prisoners from the prison tower. They retaliated by killing a white correctional officer.

Numerous popular cultural expressions associated with black power appeared at this time. Released in August 1968, the number one [Rhythm & Blues single](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_R\&B/Hip-Hop_Songs) for the [*Billboard* Year-End](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Billboard_Year-End) list was [James Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Brown)'s "[Say It Loud – I'm Black and I'm Proud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Say_It_Loud_%E2%80%93_I'm_Black_and_I'm_Proud)".[^cite_note-313] In October 1968, [Tommie Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tommie_Smith) and [John Carlos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Carlos), while being awarded the gold and bronze medals, respectively, at the [1968 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Summer_Olympics), donned human rights badges; each raised a black-gloved Black Power salute during their podium ceremony.

King was not comfortable with the "Black Power" slogan, which sounded too much like [black nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_nationalism) to him. When King was assassinated in 1968, Stokely Carmichael said that whites had murdered the one person who would prevent rampant rioting and that Black people would burn every major city to the ground. Riots broke out in more than 100 cities across the country. Some cities did not recover from the damage for more than a generation; other neighborhoods in those cities never recovered.

### Native Americans

King and the civil rights movement inspired the [Native American rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_rights_movement) of the 1960s, as well as many of its leaders.[^cite_note-Natives_King-314] Native Americans had been [dehumanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dehumanized) as "merciless Indian savages" in the [United States Declaration of Independence](31874.md),[^cite_note-315] and in King's 1964 book *[Why We Can't Wait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Why_We_Can't_Wait)* he wrote: "Our nation was born in genocide when it embraced the doctrine that the original American, the Indian, was an inferior race."[^cite_note-316] John Echohawk, a member of the [Pawnee tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pawnee_people) and the executive director and one of the founders of the [Native American Rights Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_Rights_Fund), stated: "Inspired by Dr. King, who was advancing the civil rights agenda of equality under the laws of this country, we thought that we could also use the laws to advance our Indianship, to live as tribes in our territories governed by our own laws under the principles of tribal sovereignty that had been with us ever since 1831. We believed that we could fight for a policy of self-determination that was consistent with U.S. law and that we could govern our own affairs, define our own ways and continue to survive in this society".[^cite_note-317] Native Americans were also active supporters of King's movement throughout the 1960s, which included a sizable Native American contingent at the 1963 March on Washington for Jobs and Freedom.[^cite_note-Natives_King-314]

### Northern Ireland

See also: [Northern Ireland civil rights movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Ireland_civil_rights_movement)



Mural of [Malcolm X](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malcolm_X) in [Belfast](5046.md)

Due to policies of [segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Segregation_in_Northern_Ireland) and disenfranchisement present in Northern Ireland, many Irish activists took inspiration from American civil rights activists. [People's Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Democracy_\(Ireland\)) had organized a ["Long March" from Belfast to Derry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burntollet_Bridge_incident) which was inspired by the [Selma to Montgomery marches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selma_to_Montgomery_marches).[^cite_note-kingireland-318] During the civil rights movement in Northern Ireland protesters often sang the American protest song [We Shall Overcome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/We_Shall_Overcome) and sometimes referred to themselves as the "negroes of Northern Ireland".[^cite_note-civilrightsireland-319]

### White "status quo" Southerners

See also: [The Maid Narratives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Maid_Narratives)

A majority of [White Southerners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Southerners) have been estimated to have been opposed to the civil rights movement and racial equality, though most did not engage in violence or join the Klan.[^cite_note-NPRprof-320] Many did not enjoy the idea of expanding civil rights but were uncomfortable with the language and often violent tactics used by those who resisted the civil rights movement as part of the [Massive resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massive_resistance).[^cite_note-321] Many only reacted to the movement once forced to by their changing environment, and when they did, their response was usually whatever they felt would disturb their daily life the least. Most of their personal reactions, whether eventually in support or resistance, were not extreme.[^cite_note-NPRprof-320]

### White segregationists

See also: [White supremacy in the United States](41154041.md) and [Neo-Nazism § United States](54361.md#United_States)



Ku Klux Klan demonstration in St. Augustine, Florida in 1964

King reached the height of his popularity in 1964, when he was awarded the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md). After that point, his career was filled with frustrating challenges. The [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) coalition that had gained passage of the [Civil Rights Act of 1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_Rights_Act_of_1964) and the [Voting Rights Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_Rights_Act_of_1965) began to fray.

King was becoming more estranged from the Johnson administration. In 1965, he broke with it by calling for peace negotiations and a halt to the [bombing of Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Rolling_Thunder). He moved further [left](18499.md) in the following years, speaking about the need for economic justice and thoroughgoing changes in American society. He believed that change was needed beyond the civil rights movement that had been gained.

However, King's attempts to broaden the scope of the civil rights movement were halting and largely unsuccessful. In 1965, King and James Bevel took the Movement north to address [housing discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_discrimination) in Chicago. The SCLC's Chicago campaign publicly failed because Chicago's Mayor [Richard J. Daley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_J._Daley) marginalized it by promising to "study" the city's problems. In 1966, after the Chicago open housing movement had ended, white demonstrators in notoriously racist [Cicero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cicero,_Illinois), a suburb of Chicago, held "white power" signs and threw stones at marchers who were still demonstrating against [housing segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_segregation).[^cite_note-Urgency_7-322]

Politicians and journalists quickly blamed this white [backlash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backlash_\(sociology\)) on the movement's shift towards Black Power in the mid-1960s; today most scholars believe the backlash was a phenomenon that was already developing in the mid-1950s, and it was embodied in the "[massive resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massive_resistance)" movement in the South where even the few moderate white leaders (including [George Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Wallace), who had once been endorsed by the NAACP) shifted to openly racist positions.[^cite_note-323][^cite_note-324] Northern and Western racists opposed the southerners on a regional and cultural basis, but also held segregationist attitudes which became more pronounced as the civil rights movement headed north and west. For instance, before the Watts riot, California whites had already mobilized to [repeal the state's 1963 fair housing law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_Proposition_14_\(1964\)).[^cite_note-Urgency_7-322]

Even so, the backlash at the time was unable to roll back the major civil rights victories achieved or swing the country into a reaction. Social historians Matthew Lassiter and [Barbara Ehrenreich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Ehrenreich) note that the backlash's primary constituency was [suburban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suburban) and middle-class, not working-class whites: "among the white electorate, one half of blue-collar voters ... cast their ballot for \[the liberal presidential candidate] [Hubert Humphrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/38th_Vice_President_of_the_United_States) in 1968 ... only in the South did [George Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Wallace) draw substantially more blue-collar than white-collar support."[^cite_note-325]

## In popular culture

Main article: [Civil rights movement in popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights_movement_in_popular_culture)

The 1954 to 1968 civil rights movement contributed strong cultural threads to American and international theater, song, film, television, and art.

[^cite_note-2]: &#x20;The social movement has also been called the **1960s civil rights movement**, the **African-American civil rights movement**, the **Afro-American civil rights movement**, the **American civil rights movement**, the **American freedom movement**, the **Black civil rights movement**, the **Black revolution**, the **Black rights movement**, the **civil rights revolution**, the **civil rights struggle**, the **modern civil rights movement**, the **Negro American revolution**, the **Negro freedom movement**, the **Negro movement**, the **Negro revolt**, the **Negro revolution**, the **Second Emancipation**, the **Second Reconstruction**, the **Southern freedom movement**, and the **United States civil rights movement**. *[Civil rights struggles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_rights_movement_\(disambiguation\))* can denote this or other [social movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_movements) that occurred in the United States during the same period. The social movement's span of time is called the **civil rights era**.

The summer of 1967—the "summer of love" for America's youth counterculture—was a "long hot summer" for Black urban Americans, a season of the deadliest and most widespread racial strife in US history.
